### 4.3 Handling of Missing Data for Prescription Drugs

In 2024, a number of prescription drug variables underwent statistical imputation to account for item nonresponse and, therefore, had no missing data (Section 2.3.3). Imputed variables included all variables that were used to estimate any use and misuse in the past year for the overall categories of prescription pain relievers, tranquilizers, stimulants, and sedatives and the variables for prescription fentanyl products ${ }^{79}$ and any benzodiazepine. Past year initiation variables for prescription drug misuse, corresponding to questions in Figure 4.1, and SUD

[^54]variables for prescription drugs also were imputed (see Sections 2.3.3, 3.4.6, and 3.4.7). However, prescription drug variables for the following estimates in NSDUH national reports and tables did not undergo statistical imputation and, therefore, had missing data:

- reasons for the last misuse of prescription drugs within a given category (i.e., pain relievers, tranquilizers, stimulants, or sedatives);
- sources of prescription drugs for the last misuse of drugs in a given category in the past year; and
- most subtypes of prescription drugs (e.g., hydrocodone products) that were used or misused in the past year, except for prescription fentanyl products and any benzodiazepine.

When outcomes were not imputed, respondents with missing data were excluded from analyses. Bias may result when respondents with missing data are excluded from an analysis. For population totals (i.e., estimated numbers of people with a given characteristic), a negative bias will always occur if there are missing values in the domain variables, the outcome variable, or both. For the resulting outcomes (e.g., numbers of people who obtained the last prescription drug they misused from a particular source), this negative bias can yield estimates lower than the true population total. ${ }^{80}$ When population proportions are estimated, there may or may not be bias, and the bias can be negative or positive. The direction and magnitude of the bias for proportions depend on how different the item respondents are from the item nonrespondents with respect to the outcome of interest.

Respondents could have missing data for whether they used or misused specific subtypes of prescription drugs in the past year. For example, respondents were presented with a list of prescription tranquilizers containing alprazolam and were asked to report which, if any, of these they had used in the past 12 months. Except in special situations, respondents who answered "don't know" or "refused" when presented with this list would have missing data for the past year use of alprazolam products. In turn, these respondents were not asked whether they misused specific alprazolam products in the past year. 81

The availability of "OTHER, Specify" questions for any use of other pain relievers since 2024 could affect the occurrence of missing data for pain reliever subtypes. As for other prescription drug categories, respondents could answer "don't know" or "refused" to questions in the pain

[^55]relievers screener section that showed a specific list of pain relievers they used in the past year. However, respondents could specify use of these drugs as other pain relievers they used for any reason in the past year. For example, if respondents answered "don't know" or "refused" to the question about any use of hydrocodone products in the past year but they specified a hydrocodone product (e.g., Vicodin ${ }^{\circledR}$ ) was one of the other prescription pain relievers they used in the past year, then these respondents logically used hydrocodone products in that period. Hence, these respondents no longer had missing data for the use of hydrocodone products in the past year. This special situation was in addition to the opportunity that respondents had to specify misuse of pain relievers in a given subtype despite having missing data for any use of pain relievers in that subtype.

Beginning in 2021, if variables for the use and misuse of prescription drug subtypes had missing data, respondents with missing values were excluded from analyses. Statistical imputation and excluding respondents with missing data for prescription drug subtype variables were adopted because missing data rates were low for most prescription drug variables. For example, see Table 2.4 in the 2022 National Survey on Drug Use and Health (NSDUH): Methodological Summary and Definitions (CBHSQ, 2023). Remaining prescription drug variables that were not included in national reports and tables for the 2024 NSDUH were edited but not imputed. Potential biases associated with missing data discussed in this section and in Section 3.3.2 will apply to analyses using these edited variables.

### 4.4 Measures of Opioid Misuse and Use in NSDUH

### 4.4.1 Background

The subtypes of opioid pain relievers described in Section 4.2.2 are available in the United States by prescription as controlled substances. As opioids, however, they can produce the same kinds of adverse effects as heroin or other illegally manufactured opioids. People who misuse prescription opioids can develop an opioid use disorder or can overdose, sometimes fatally. According to the DSM-5 SUD criteria, people who are prescribed opioids for pain relief and take them under medical supervision also can develop opioid use disorder. ${ }^{82}$ As noted in Section 4.2.2, most prescription opioids in the NSDUH questionnaire are in the more stringently controlled Schedule II category because of their high potential for abuse that can lead to severe psychological or physiological dependence.

NSDUH respondents were asked about their use and misuse of prescription pain relievers rather than being asked specifically about their use and misuse of prescription opioids. Respondents were assumed to be more likely to understand the term "pain relievers" rather than "opioids" because "pain relievers" indicates the purpose for which the drugs are likely to be taken. In

[^56]contrast to "pain relievers," the term "opioids" could be too sophisticated for respondents at a 6th grade reading level. This term also would require respondents to know the chemical classification of a prescription drug. An online survey in 2020 of about 1,000 adult respondents in the United States indicated respondent difficulty in differentiating between medications that were opioids or nonopioids (Duff et al., 2021; DrFirst, 2020).

The 2024 NSDUH questionnaire included questions about 38 specific prescription pain relievers that fall into 11 opioid pain reliever subtypes (see Section 4.2.2 and Table 4.1). As noted in Section 4.1, however, respondents also were asked whether they used or misused any other prescription pain reliever in the past 12 months. Beginning in 2024, respondents who reported any use of other pain relievers in the past 12 months were asked to report the names of the other drugs they used, in addition to being asked to report the names of the other drugs they misused.

However, the general prescription pain reliever category includes prescription drugs that are not opioids, such as prescription strengths of nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (e.g., prescription-strength ibuprofen or naproxen). If NSDUH respondents reported using or misusing other prescription pain relievers in the past 12 months, these other pain relievers might not be opioids. Therefore, published NSDUH estimates of the use and misuse of prescription pain relievers are not completely synonymous with the use and misuse of prescription opioids.

The number of prescriptions for opioids has declined since the release of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) 2016 opioid prescribing guideline (Bohnert et al., 2018; Dowell et al., 2016), and there have been modest increases in the prescribing of nonopioids for chronic pain (Goldstick et al., 2022). The CDC updated clinical practice guideline in 2022 for prescribing opioids for chronic pain noted that nonopioid therapies are at least as effective as opioids for many types of acute pain, defined as pain lasting less than 1 month. The 2022 guideline also recommended nonopioid therapies as the preferred treatment for pain that is considered subacute (lasting 1 to 3 months) or chronic (lasting more than 3 months) (Dowell et al., 2022).

Because of these clinical practice guidelines and observed changes in medication prescribing for pain relief, some nonopioid pain relievers could emerge as drugs of abuse. For example, nonopioid drugs such as gabapentin (brand name Neurontin ${ }^{\circledR}$ ) that are prescribed "off label" ${ }^{83}$ for pain relief may have abuse potential, especially among people with a history of opioid misuse (Buttram, 2018; Buttram et al., 2017; Evoy et al., 2021; Havens, 2018; Smith et al., 2016). Detection of gabapentin increased in fatal drug overdoses in 2019-2020, but nearly 90 percent of the overdose deaths in which gabapentin was detected also involved opioids (Mattson et al., 2022). Use of gabapentin with opioids has been associated with an increased risk of overdose compared with the use of opioids alone (Chou et al., 2020). Other adverse

[^57]effects associated with gabapentin include blurred vision, cognitive effects, and sedation (McDonagh et al., 2020). In 2024, the first year in which data have been available for the specific other prescription pain relievers that respondents used for any reason in the past year, nearly 250 respondents reported that gabapentin was another pain reliever that they used in the past year. These reports are likely to underestimate the true prevalence of use of this drug (Kroutil et al., 2010).

### 4.4.2 Prescription Pain Reliever and Prescription Opioid Misuse

NSDUH respondents who reported the misuse of only any other prescription pain reliever are counted in NSDUH estimates of prescription pain reliever misuse, regardless of whether the other drugs they misused are opioids or nonopioids. Beginning with the 2024 NSDUH, published estimates are presented for the misuse of prescription opioids in the past 12 months. Prior analyses of NSDUH data have indicated that a small percentage of pain reliever misuse estimates can be attributed to respondents who explicitly reported the misuse of only nonopioids. For more information, see the 2023 Methodological Summary and Definitions report (CBHSQ, 2024b).

Figure 4.7, which refers to the "Pain Relievers Main Section" of the NSDUH questionnaire, describes the information that was used for creating variables to estimate the misuse of prescription opioids in the past year.

- Respondents who reported the past year misuse of any of the prescription opioids in the questionnaire (Table 4.1 and Figure 4.3) were classified as having misused prescription opioids in that period. ${ }^{84}$
- The following respondents who reported the past year misuse of only other pain relievers were classified as having misused prescription opioids: ${ }^{\mathbf{8 4}}$
- those who specified the misuse of prescription opioids as other pain relievers, including situations in which respondents specified the misuse of prescription opioids in the questionnaire despite not having reported their misuse;
- those who did not know or refused to specify the names of other pain relievers they misused; and
- those who provided a nonspecific answer, such as "painkillers," without indicating the specific pain reliever(s) they misused.

Consistent with the procedures described in Section 4.1.2, responses of "don't know," "refused," or a nonspecific other pain reliever were assumed to be potential indications of the misuse of prescription opioids. Respondents who reported that they misused only other prescription pain relievers in the past 12 months were classified as not having misused prescription opioids if the only pain relievers they specified misusing were nonopioid drugs. If respondents did not provide sufficient information to establish whether they misused

[^58]prescription opioids in the past year, statistical imputation was used to classify respondents as having misused or not having misused prescription opioids in the past year.

Pain Relievers Main Section

Figure 4.7 Classification of the Misuse of Prescription Opioids in the Past Year
![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/2025_09_14_e1b0b5c397f8f677419eg-137.jpg?height=1464&width=1568&top_left_y=504&top_left_x=284)

The percentage of people aged 12 or older who misused prescription opioids in the past year decreased from 3.0 percent in 2021 to 2.6 percent in 2024. For more information, see the Key Substance Use and Mental Health Indicators in the United States: Results from the 2024 National Survey on Drug Use and Health report (CBHSQ, 2025g). About 95 percent of people in each year who misused any prescription pain reliever in the past year were classified as having misused prescription opioids.

### 4.4.3 Any Opioid Misuse, Including Heroin

In national reports and tables for the 2024 NSDUH, the categories for the misuse of prescription opioids and the use of heroin were combined into an overall category for opioid misuse. As shown in the 2024 Key Substance Use and Mental Health Indicators report, most misuse of opioids in the past year involves the misuse of prescription opioids rather than heroin use (CBHSQ, 2025g). Therefore, including data from respondents who used heroin in the past year does not appreciably affect estimates of any opioid misuse.

The percentage of people aged 12 or older who misused any opioid in the past year (i.e., prescription opioids or heroin) decreased from 3.2 percent in 2021 to 2.7 percent in 2024, as discussed in the 2024 Key Substance Use and Mental Health Indicators report (CBHSQ, 2025 g ). Including data from respondents who used heroin in the past year changed the estimates in 2021 from 3.0 percent for prescription opioid misuse to 3.2 percent for any opioid misuse, including heroin (Section 4.4.2). Likewise, estimates in 2024 changed from 2.6 percent for prescription opioid misuse to 2.7 percent for any opioid misuse, including heroin.

### 4.4.4 Any Opioid Misuse, Including Heroin and Illegally Made Fentanyl

Beginning with the 2022 NSDUH, respondents aged 12 or older were asked whether they ever used IMF and, if so, how long it had been since they last used it. The questionnaire explained that IMF is fentanyl that people cannot get from a doctor or pharmacy and that IMF can come in forms such as powder, pills, blotter paper, or mixed with heroin or other drugs. Therefore, the categories for the misuse of prescription opioids and the use of heroin or IMF can be used to create an overall category for opioid misuse that includes IMF; corresponding estimates are not available for 2021.

Similar to the opioid misuse measure discussed in Section 4.4.3, including data from respondents who used heroin or IMF in the past year did not appreciably affect estimates of any opioid misuse. In 2024, for example, estimates for the misuse of opioids among people aged 12 or older were 2.7 percent if data were not included from respondents who used IMF in the past year (Section 4.4.3) and 2.8 percent if data were included from respondents who used IMF.

### 4.4.5 Any Past Year Prescription Pain Reliever and Prescription Opioid Use

Figure 4.8, which refers to the "Pain Relievers Screener Section" of the NSDUH questionnaire, describes the information that was used for creating variables to estimate any use of prescription opioids in the past year. Logically, if respondents were classified as having misused prescription opioids in the past year, they also used prescription opioids for any reason in that
period. If respondents were classified as not having misused prescription opioids in the past year, they still could have used prescription opioids in the past year.

Pain Relievers Screener Section

Figure 4.8 Classification of the Use of Prescription Opioids in the Past Year
![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/2025_09_14_e1b0b5c397f8f677419eg-139.jpg?height=1635&width=1383&top_left_y=485&top_left_x=371)

Rx = prescription.
NOTE: As per Figure 4.1, respondents were first asked about any use of specific Rx pain relievers in the past 12 months. To illustrate the logic for identifying the use and misuse of prescription opioids, the pain relievers main section is presented in Figure 4.7. Logically, if respondents misused Rx opioids in the past 12 months, then they also used them for any reason in that period.
${ }^{1}$ Classification of respondents as having used but not misused Rx opioids applied if respondents did not report misuse in the past 12 months.
${ }^{2}$ Respondents in 2024 were assumed to have used Rx opioids if they did not know or refused to report the other pain relievers they used or they reported a nonspecific drug (e.g., "painkillers"). Respondents were classified as "unknown" for the use of Rx opioids as other pain relievers if they did not know or refused to report whether they used other pain relievers in the past 12 months.

If respondents were not classified as having misused prescription opioids in the past year, the following information was used to identify whether they had used prescription opioids for any reason in the past year:

- Respondents who reported the past year use of any of the prescription opioids in the questionnaire (Table 4.1 and Figure 4.3) were classified as having used prescription opioids in that period.
- Respondents in 2021-2023 who did not use any of the prescription opioids in the questionnaire were classified as not having used prescription opioids in the past year. Reports of the use of only other prescription pain relievers were assumed to involve the use of only nonopioids.
- For respondents in 2024, information on the other pain relievers that respondents specified for any use in the past year was available for classifying respondents as having used prescription opioids. Specifically, the following respondents were classified as having used prescription opioids in the past year:
- those who specified the use of prescription opioids as other pain relievers, including situations in which respondents specified the use of prescription opioids in the questionnaire despite not having reported their misuse;
- those who did not know or refused to specify the names of other pain relievers they used; and
- those who provided a nonspecific answer, such as "painkillers," without indicating the specific pain reliever(s) they used.

If respondents in 2021-2023 did not report the misuse of prescription opioids, reported the use of only other prescription pain relievers, and definitely did not use pain relievers in any of the 11 prescription opioid subtypes, $\frac{85}{5}$ then the most conservative approach was to assume that the other prescription pain relievers were all nonopioid drugs, except when respondents were assumed to have misused opioids in the past year, as noted previously (see Figure 4.7). Respondents in 2024 who did not report the misuse of prescription opioids, reported the use of only other prescription pain relievers, and definitely did not use pain relievers in any of the 11 prescription opioid subtypes were classified as not having used prescription opioids if the only pain relievers they specified using were nonopioid drugs.

If respondents in 2021-2024 did not provide sufficient information to establish whether they used prescription opioids in the past year, statistical imputation was used to classify respondents as having used or not having used prescription opioids in the past year. For example, opioid pain reliever use could be unknown if respondents did not report use in the past year of specific pain relievers in the 11 prescription opioid subtypes in the questionnaire, but they had missing data for any of these subtypes.

[^59]About 1 in 5 people aged 12 or older in 2021-2023 were assumed to have used prescription opioids in the past year because they reported using prescription opioids in the NSDUH questionnaire or were assumed to have misused prescription opioids. In 2024, about 1 in 5 people aged 12 or older ( 21.9 percent) also were estimated to have used prescription opioids in the past year.

Tests of the linear trend for prescription opioid use in 2021-2024 among people aged 12 or older indicated no change over this period. Testing was not conducted for a trend in the percentage of past year users of prescription pain relievers who used prescription opioids because the measure in 2024 of any use of prescription pain relievers in the past year was not comparable with the measure in 2021 to 2023 (see Section 4.1.5.2).

### 4.4.6 Any Past Year Opioid Use Including Heroin

As for opioid misuse, factoring in reports of heroin use in the past year did not appreciably change the estimates of any past year use of opioids compared with the estimates in the preceding section for any past year use of prescription opioids. In 2024, for example, the estimate for any past year use of opioids including heroin also was 21.9 percent.

### 4.4.7 Any Past Year Opioid Use Including Heroin and Illegally Made Fentanyl

With the addition of new questions for 2022 about the use of IMF, an overall category has been created for any opioid use that included any past year use of prescription opioids, heroin, or IMF, in addition to measures for any past year use of opioids that included prescription opioids or heroin. However, factoring in reports of IMF use in the past year did not appreciably change the estimates of any past year use of opioids compared with the estimates in preceding sections for any past year use of prescription opioids or any past year use of opioids including heroin. In 2024, for example, estimates for the use of opioids among people aged 12 or older were 21.9 percent if data were not included from respondents who used IMF in the past year (see Sections 4.4.5 and 4.4.6) and 22.0 percent if data were included from respondents who used IMF.

### 4.4.8 Opioid Use Disorder in the Past Year

Although reports of the use and misuse of prescription pain relievers could include drugs that are not prescription opioids, SUD questions for prescription pain relievers in the NSDUH interview apply to DSM-5 criteria for opioid use disorder. Therefore, beginning with the 2024 NSDUH, respondents were not counted as having an opioid use disorder in the past year if they used prescription pain relievers but not prescription opioids in the past year and they did not use heroin in the past year. See Section 3.4.7 for information on how opioid use disorder is defined. Because questions about the use of IMF in the past year are asked in a later section of the interview, after respondents have been asked SUD questions, estimates of opioid use disorder take into account disorder symptoms for heroin or prescription opioids but not IMF.

Excluding data from respondents who used only nonopioid pain relievers and did not use heroin in the past year did not affect conclusions about trends in opioid use disorder and had minimal effect on estimates. Percentages of people aged 12 or older who were classified as having a
heroin use disorder or a pain reliever use disorder ${ }^{86}$ showed no change from 2021 to 2024. There also was no change from 2021 to 2024 for percentages of people aged 12 or older with an opioid use disorder after data had been excluded from respondents who used only nonopioid pain relievers and did not use heroin in the past year. Among people aged 12 or older in 2024, 1.8 percent were classified as having a heroin use disorder or pain reliever use disorder, and 1.7 percent were classified as having an opioid use disorder.

### 4.5 Measures of Tranquilizer or Sedative Use and Misuse in NSDUH

### 4.5.1 Background

Drugs defined in NSDUH as tranquilizers or sedatives have a number of important features in common.

- Both are CNS depressants, and, therefore, both cause drowsiness (NIDA, 2023).
- Most have a common effect on specific activity in the brain (NIDA, 2023). 87
- Most are classified in Schedule IV under the CSA (Tables 4.3 and 4.4).
- Chemically similar drugs (e.g., benzodiazepines) are prescribed as either tranquilizers or sedatives.

The Multum Lexicon ${ }^{\circledR}$ database of drugs has a category for "anxiolytics, sedatives, and hypnotics" that includes drugs defined in NSDUH as tranquilizers or sedatives (National Center for Health Statistics, 2023). Because of these similarities, national reports and tables for the 2023 NSDUH include estimates for the misuse of any tranquilizer or sedative.

### 4.5.2 Creation of Measures for Tranquilizer or Sedative Misuse

The following measures for the misuse of tranquilizers or sedatives were included in reports or tables for the 2024 NSDUH:

- misuse of tranquilizers or sedatives in the past 12 months (i.e., past year),
- misuse of tranquilizers or sedatives in the past 30 days (i.e., past month), and
- tranquilizer or sedative use disorder in the past 12 months.


### 4.5.2.1 Past Year or Past Month Misuse of Tranquilizers or Sedatives

Respondents were classified as having misused prescription tranquilizers or sedatives in the past 12 months if they reported the misuse of prescription tranquilizers, prescription sedatives, or both in that period. A similar principle applied to the classification of respondents as having misused tranquilizers or sedatives in the past 30 days.

[^60]As previously noted, respondents who reported that they misused other tranquilizers in the past 12 months were asked to specify the names of the other tranquilizers they misused. Similarly, respondents who reported that they misused other sedatives in that period were asked to specify the names of the other sedatives they misused. Figure 4.9 describes how data were handled if respondents specified a prescription tranquilizer (e.g., Xanax ${ }^{\circledR}$ ) as some "other sedative" they misused, or vice versa. Consistent with the principle discussed in Section 2.3.2.1, if respondents specified the misuse of a prescription tranquilizer as some other sedative they had misused, for example, these data for the misuse of other sedatives were not used to edit the data for the use and misuse of tranquilizers in that section of the interview. However, the reporting of prescription tranquilizers as other sedatives or the reporting of prescription sedatives as other tranquilizers did not affect the creation of the aggregate measures for the misuse of prescription tranquilizers or sedatives in the past year or past month.

Figure 4.9 Classification of Misuse of Tranquilizers or Sedatives in the Past 12 Months Based on Cross-Reporting of Tranquilizers or Sedatives as "Other" Drugs
![](https://cdn.mathpix.com/cropped/2025_09_14_e1b0b5c397f8f677419eg-143.jpg?height=1188&width=1291&top_left_y=1062&top_left_x=417)

### 4.5.2.2 Tranquilizer or Sedative Use Disorder

Beginning in 2021, respondents were classified as having a tranquilizer or sedative use disorder in the past 12 months if they had a tranquilizer use disorder related to any use of prescription tranquilizers in the past year, a sedative use disorder related to any use of prescription sedatives in the past year, or both disorders. The SUD criteria for these substances are described in Section 3.4.7.

### 4.5.3 Estimates Not Created for Tranquilizer or Sedative Use and Misuse

The following estimates for the use or misuse of any tranquilizer or sedative were not created for the 2024 national reports and tables; these estimates could be created for prescription pain relievers and prescription stimulants:

- initiation of the misuse of tranquilizers or sedatives in the past year, and
- frequency of misuse of any tranquilizer or sedative in the past month.

As discussed in Section 3.4.6.2, the potential underreporting of lifetime (but not past year) misuse of prescription drugs could result in some people being misclassified as having initiated the misuse of any prescription tranquilizer or sedative in the past year, when in fact they first misused any prescription tranquilizer or sedative more than 12 months prior to the interview date. Therefore, aggregate estimates were not created for the 2024 NSDUH for the initiation of misuse of any tranquilizer or sedative.

Aggregate estimates for the frequency of misuse of tranquilizers or sedatives in the past 30 days were not created because these data are not mutually exclusive for respondents who misused both tranquilizers and sedatives in that period. For these respondents, the number of days they misused prescription tranquilizers or sedatives in the past 30 days cannot be summed to estimate the total number of days respondents misused tranquilizers or sedatives because respondents could have misused both prescription drug types on the same day.

### 4.6 Measures of Benzodiazepine Use and Misuse in NSDUH

### 4.6.1 Background

As noted in Sections 4.2.4, 4.2.5, and 4.5, prescription drugs categorized as benzodiazepines can be prescribed as either tranquilizers or sedatives. The benzodiazepines listed in Tables 4.3 and 4.4 are classified as Schedule IV controlled substances. Therefore, these drugs have the potential to produce physical or psychological dependence. Because benzodiazepines are CNS depressants, they cause drowsiness and can impair motor skills important for tasks such as operating a motor vehicle or machinery. People also can overdose on benzodiazepines, especially when taken in combination with other CNS depressants such as opioids (NIDA, 2023, 2024b).

Estimates were included in 2024 NSDUH national reports and tables for the use and misuse of any benzodiazepine in the past 12 months, regardless of whether benzodiazepines were classified as tranquilizers or sedatives. The next section discusses the creation of measures from the NSDUH data for any benzodiazepine use and misuse.

### 4.6.2 Creation of Measures for Benzodiazepine Use and Misuse

Respondents were classified as having used any benzodiazepine tranquilizer or sedative in the past 12 months if they reported the use of one or more of the benzodiazepines shown in Figures 4.5 and 4.6. As noted in Section 4.3, variables for the past year use or misuse of any benzodiazepine were statistically imputed for 2024 (see Section 2.3.3).

Figure 4.10 presents information on how measures of benzodiazepine use and misuse were created for NSDUH. Respondents who reported use of specific benzodiazepines included in the tranquilizers section or sedatives section in the past 12 months were classified as having used benzodiazepines for any reason in the past 12 months. Respondents also were classified as having used benzodiazepines for any reason in the past 12 months if they specified the misuse of benzodiazepines as other tranquilizers or sedatives they misused in that period. Logically, respondents who misused other benzodiazepines in the past 12 months used them for any reason. Similarly, respondents who reported they misused a benzodiazepine tranquilizer or sedative in the past 12 months-either from a response to a direct question (e.g., the direct question about misuse of Xanax ${ }^{\circledR}$ in the past 12 months) or as some other tranquilizer or sedative they misused in that period-were classified as having misused any benzodiazepine in the past 12 months. Consistent with the principle of not editing across sections of the interview (see Section 2.3.2.1), however, reports of benzodiazepines in sections other than tranquilizers or sedatives were not included in the measures of benzodiazepine use or misuse.

In addition, estimates in the 2024 Detailed Tables (CBHSQ, 2025h) for the use and misuse of any benzodiazepine tranquilizer and specific benzodiazepine tranquilizer subtypes in Fiqure 4.5 were based solely on reports from the tranquilizers section of the interview. Similarly, estimates for the use and misuse of any benzodiazepine sedative and specific benzodiazepine sedative subtypes shown in Figure 4.6 were based solely on reports from the sedatives section. For example, if respondents did not report the past year use or misuse of Xanax ${ }^{\circledR}$ in the tranquilizers section of the interview, they were not counted as having used or misused a benzodiazepine tranquilizer or an alprazolam product, even if they specified the past year misuse of Xanax ${ }^{\circledR}$ as some other sedative. However, respondents in this example were classified in a category for the past year misuse of any miscellaneous prescription benzodiazepine for the 2024 Detailed Tables. The miscellaneous prescription benzodiazepine estimates were intended to provide data users with an indication of the extent of reporting of benzodiazepines across the respective categories for tranquilizers and sedatives.

Any Past Year Use

### 4.6.3 Estimates Not Created for Benzodiazepine Use and Misuse

The following measures and associated estimates were not created for benzodiazepines:

- lifetime use or misuse of benzodiazepines,
- misuse of any benzodiazepine in the past month,
- initiation of benzodiazepine misuse in the past year, and
- an SUD attributable to the use of benzodiazepines in the past year.

These estimates were not created because the interview sections for tranquilizers and sedatives also included drugs that are not benzodiazepines.

Aside from the potential for respondents to underreport lifetime use or misuse of tranquilizers or sedatives (see Section 3.3.3.2), measures for the lifetime use or misuse of benzodiazepines could not be created because respondents who did not report use or misuse of tranquilizers or sedatives in the past 12 months were not asked whether they ever used or misused benzodiazepines. Similarly, if respondents reported that they misused any prescription tranquilizer or sedative in the past 30 days, they could have misused drugs that are not benzodiazepines (e.g., Ambien ${ }^{\circledR}$ ).

As noted in Section 3.4.6, NSDUH respondents in 2024 were asked about the initiation of misuse of prescription psychotherapeutic drugs only for the individual prescription drugs they had misused in the past 12 months. If respondents reported past year initiation of misuse for all tranquilizers or sedatives they misused in that period, they were not asked whether they ever misused benzodiazepine tranquilizers or sedatives more than 12 months prior to the interview. Consequently, past year initiation of the misuse of benzodiazepines could not be determined.

Similar to the issue described previously for the misuse of benzodiazepines in the past 30 days, past year users of tranquilizers or sedatives since 2021 have been asked, respectively, about SUD symptoms attributable to their use of any tranquilizer or any sedative in the past 12 months. ${ }^{88}$ Thus, if respondents reported the use of benzodiazepine sedatives and sedatives that were not benzodiazepines in the past 12 months, it could not be determined whether the SUD symptoms they reported applied to the benzodiazepine sedatives or the sedatives that were not benzodiazepines.

## Acknowledgments

This National Survey on Drug Use and Health (NSDUH) report was prepared by the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration (SAMHSA), Center for Behavioral Health Statistics and Quality (CBHSQ), and by RTI International. Work by RTI was performed under contract number 75S20322C00001. Carlos Graham served as contracting officer representative, and David Hunter served as RTI project director.

This report was drafted by RTI and reviewed at SAMHSA.


[^0]:    ${ }^{1}$ RTI International is a trade name of Research Triangle Institute. RTI and the RTI logo are U.S. registered trademarks of Research Triangle Institute.

[^1]:    ${ }^{\underline{2}}$ The most recent state-level estimates are for 2022-2023. The most recent substate estimates are for 2016-2018.

[^2]:    ${ }^{3}$ See the 2024 National Survey on Drug Use and Health (NSDUH) Methodological Resource Book, Section 2: Sample Design and Experience Report (CBHSQ, forthcoming b) for specifics about the ABS coverage criteria.
    ${ }^{4}$ Some large census block groups required subsampling to make field enumeration feasible.

[^3]:    Source: SAMHSA, Center for Behavioral Health Statistics and Quality, National Survey on Drug Use and Health, 2024.

[^4]:    ${ }^{5}$ If a street address could not be determined, SDUs were not sent an introductory letter.
    ${ }^{6}$ See the 2023 National Survey on Drug Use and Health (NSDUH) Methodological Resource Book, Section 8: Data Collection Final Report (CBHSQ, 2025b) for details on in-person screening for households in which members spoke only Spanish or when a language other than English or Spanish was encountered.

[^5]:    ${ }^{7}$ For more details on adaptations for web interviews starting with the 2020 NSDUH, see Section 2.2.2.4 in the 2020 National Survey on Drug Use and Health (NSDUH): Methodological Summary and Definitions (CBHSQ, 2021b).
    ${ }^{8}$ The question about whether adolescents spoke with at least one parent in the past year about the dangers of substance use was removed from the 2024 questionnaire. It has been added back to the 2025 questionnaire.

[^6]:    ${ }^{9}$ Changes to the alcohol and drug treatment and mental health service utilization sections were presented to respondents starting in June 2024.

[^7]:    ${ }^{1}$ The usable interview rule removes interviews with excessive amounts of missing data in the initial set of substance use questions.
    ${ }^{2}$ The raw data file consists of one record for each interview with no logical editing or other corrections.
    ${ }^{3}$ Some interview records were not treated as final respondents because the patterns of answers in the substance use sections called into question the validity of the data. See Section 2.3.1 for details.
    ${ }^{4}$ Analysis weights are used in analyses to make the estimates representative of the target population.
    ${ }^{5}$ Statistical imputation replaces missing, inexact, or nonspecific values in key variables.

[^8]:    10 A gate question is an initial question or set of questions that ask whether the behavior or characteristic of interest applies to the respondent. An affirmative response to a gate question leads to respondents being asked a series of other related questions. A response other than an affirmative one to all relevant gate questions results in respondents skipping additional questions on that topic and being routed to the next set of topics in the interview.
    ${ }^{11}$ For example, answers of " 1 " wherever possible in the sections for marijuana, cocaine, crack, heroin, and methamphetamine would mean that respondents used that substance in their lifetime, first used the substance at age 1 , last used in the past 30 days, preferred to answer the 12-month frequency question as an average number of days per week in the past 12 months, used an average of 1 day per week in the past 12 months, and used on 1 day in the past 30 days.

[^9]:    ${ }^{12}$ In the 2024 NSDUH, misuse was defined as use "in any way a doctor did not direct you to use [it or them]" and focused on behaviors that constitute misuse of prescription drugs. Examples of misuse were presented to respondents and included (1) use without a prescription of the respondent's own; (2) use in greater amounts, more often, or longer than told to take a drug; and (3) use in any other way a doctor did not direct the respondent to use a drug.

[^10]:    ${ }^{13}$ This instruction was not included for prescription tranquilizers because no tranquilizers are available in the United States without a prescription.

[^11]:    14 "OTHER, Specify" questions were added to the pain reliever screener section of the questionnaire in 2024, allowing respondents to report which "other" pain relievers they had used in the past 12 months. ${ }^{15}$ In the 2024 NSDUH, drug use included use of marijuana, cocaine (including crack), heroin, hallucinogens, inhalants, or methamphetamine or any use of prescription stimulants, tranquilizers or sedatives (e.g., benzodiazepines), or pain relievers.

[^12]:    16 Person-level analysis weights refer to the weights used to produce population estimates from final survey respondents' data. Other special weights are also produced for NSDUH (e.g., pair weights for analysis of data from pairs of responding household members), but this report does not discuss the creation of these special weights.
    ${ }^{17}$ Starting with the 2022 NSDUH, an adjustment was made to account for the proportions of interviews completed via the web or in person. These adjustments also were applied to the original 2021 weight to produce an updated version of that weight to facilitate comparisons between years. This updated 2021 analysis weight was used for all SAMHSA reports published in 2024 that include 2021 data.

[^13]:    ${ }^{1}$ Poststratification of household weights to meet population controls for various household-level demographics was done to obtain census-consistent estimates based on the household rosters from all screened households.
    ${ }^{2}$ This adjustment poststratified the weights of selected household members to conform to the adjusted roster estimates. This step took advantage of the separate screening and interviewing nature of the NSDUH design.

[^14]:    ${ }^{18}$ Web-based data collection yielded more sample dwelling units (SDUs) with unknown eligibility because of nonresponse from SDUs, and in-person contact attempts were not successful for determining eligibility.

[^15]:    19 Analyses conducted for the 2021 NSDUH indicated that key substance use and mental health estimates were subject to mode effects. See Chapter 6 in the 2021 Methodological Summary and Definitions report (CBHSQ, 2022) for more information on these analyses.

[^16]:    ${ }^{20}$ See the estimated total U.S. population and estimated number of people in households averaged over 5 years in Table B09019.
    ${ }^{21}$ See the estimated group quarters population by type averaged over 5 years in Table B26203.

[^17]:    ${ }^{22}$ This "mixed-method" approach applies only to SEs for estimated numbers of people. All SEs for proportions and means are calculated directly in SUDAAN.
    ${ }^{23}$ In some years, not all of the race domains in Table 3.1 are forced to fully match the U.S. Census Bureau population estimates due to some models not converging. Even when race domains do not fully match the U.S. Census Bureau population estimates, the sampling variation for these domains is considered negligible. Therefore, the same race domains are considered fixed for every year.

[^18]:    ${ }^{25}$ Pairwise significance testing between 2 years of data is not the same as linear tests of trends that involve testing for changes across 4 or more consecutive years of comparable data, including the baseline year.
    ${ }^{26}$ The test statistic $t$ is a ratio with the numerator defined as the difference between two prevalence estimates. The denominator is the standard deviation of this difference due to sampling variability. The denominator also takes into account when estimates are not independent.

[^19]:    ${ }^{27}$ The degrees of freedom for most statistical tests are typically calculated as the number of primary sampling units (variance replicates) minus the number of strata. Because there are two replicates per stratum, 750 degrees of freedom equal the number of strata in the national sample for 2024. However, the degrees of freedom are smaller for some statistical comparisons; specifically, the degrees of freedom are reduced for estimates on the average number of days people used substances. Details can be found in the 2023 National Survey on Drug Use and Health (NSDUH) Methodological Resource Book, Section 13: Statistical Inference Report (CBHSQ, 2025d).

[^20]:    ${ }^{28}$ Other statistical methods have been used for comparisons of pairwise differences across three or more levels of a categorical variable once an overall test (such as Shah's $F$ ) suggests there are differences. Although a Bonferroni adjustment can be applied to every pairwise difference (i.e., and not just to the pairwise difference with the lowest $p$ value, which is sometimes recommended instead of Shah's $F$ as an alternative overall test), this is an overly conservative procedure. For example, if a $p$ value of .05 is set as the criterion for statistical significance and there are three pairwise comparisons, then the Bonferroniadjusted $p$ value for statistical significance becomes .017 (i.e., .05 divided by 3 equals .017 ).

[^21]:    ${ }^{29}$ A dwelling unit (DU) in NSDUH refers to either a housing unit or a group quarters listing unit, such as a dormitory room or a shelter bed.
    ${ }^{30}$ The DU weight is the number of DUs in the population represented by each sampled DU. Thus, the weighted screening response rate (SRR) is the estimated population-level SRR.
    ${ }^{31}$ A successfully screened DU is one in which all screening questionnaire items were answered by an adult resident of the DU and either zero, one, or two DU members were selected for the NSDUH interview.

[^22]:    32 As an example of skip logic, respondents were asked questions regarding alcohol or drug use treatment only if they previously reported lifetime use of any of these substances. If respondents did not report lifetime use, then questions about locations where substance use treatment was received were skipped. If respondents did not report lifetime use for any substances but did not know or refused to report lifetime use for some substances, then variables corresponding to these skipped questions had missing data.

[^23]:    33 People who started the survey but broke off the interview before completing a minimum number of drug use questions were not kept as final respondents because of the usability criteria described in Section 2.3.1.
    ${ }^{34}$ See Section 3.4.10.1 for the definition of mental health treatment.
    ${ }^{35}$ See Section 3.4.8.1 for the definition of substance use treatment.

[^24]:    ${ }^{36}$ Results of this study showed an 84.6 percent agreement between self-reported tobacco use in the past 30 days and urine drug test results for tobacco. For marijuana, there was 89.8 percent agreement between self-reported use in the past 30 days and urine drug test results, although this agreement was dominated by people who reported no use and tested negative ( 82.9 percent).

[^25]:    ${ }^{37}$ The exception was for the pain reliever OxyContin ${ }^{\circledR}$ (an extended-release formulation of oxycodone).
    ${ }^{38}$ In the revised version of the race question, Native Hawaiian, Samoan, and Other Pacific Islander were part of a single category rather than three categories as in years prior to 2022. However, more detailed information was collected in a new follow-up question and corresponding "OTHER, Specify" question.

[^26]:    ${ }^{39}$ See the fentanyl drug fact sheet.

[^27]:    ${ }^{40}$ Nicotine vaping measures in 2022 through 2024 are not comparable with corresponding measures from 2021 because the nicotine vaping questions in 2021 were asked in the later emerging issues section. For additional details, see Chapter 3 in the 2022 Methodological Summary and Definitions report (CBHSQ, 2023).

[^28]:    ${ }^{41}$ Section 3.4.13 discusses reporting of estimates for adolescent suicidality when adolescents did not know how to answer the questions.
    ${ }^{42}$ Respondents beginning in 2022 were asked about any use of prescription psychotherapeutic drugs. Any use includes use of medication as directed with a prescription of the individual's own or misuse of prescription psychotherapeutics. However, respondents were not asked when they first used psychotherapeutics for any reason. Therefore, initiation for psychotherapeutics in NSDUH refers to the first time people misused these medications rather than the first time they used these medications for any reason.
    43 For brevity, "misuse" is not repeated whenever the text refers to first use. Terms such as "past year use" and "first use" used in the remainder of this chapter for substance use in general refer to misuse for prescription psychotherapeutic drugs.

[^29]:    ${ }^{44}$ Other potential aggregate categories for initiation that included prescription drugs were tranquilizers or sedatives as a combined category, benzodiazepines, opioids (i.e., heroin or prescription pain relievers), and CNS stimulants (i.e., cocaine, methamphetamine, or prescription stimulants).
    ${ }^{45}$ This example where respondents reported past year initiation of heroin use but failed to report lifetime misuse of prescription pain relievers also would apply to the measure for past year initiation of opioid misuse.
    ${ }^{46}$ The Results from the 2024 National Survey on Drug Use and Health: Detailed Tables (CBHSQ, 2025h) also do not show initiation estimates for the aggregate category of illicit drugs other than marijuana.

[^30]:    ${ }^{47}$ Respondents also were asked the follow-up question if the sum of the reports of past year initiation plus missing data for initiation equaled the number of specific drugs they misused in the past year (and there were no reports of initiation of misuse more than 12 months prior to the interview date).

[^31]:    48 People who never misused prescription drugs remain "at risk" of initiation. Therefore, respondents who underreported the lifetime (but not past year) misuse of prescription drugs could be misclassified as still being at risk of initiation for the misuse of prescription drugs.

[^32]:    ${ }^{49}$ NSDUH respondents in 2024 were asked the respective questions for alcohol use disorder or marijuana use disorder if they reported use of these substances on 6 or more days in the past year. Respondents were asked SUD questions for other substances if they reported any use in the past year.

[^33]:    50 For alcohol, for example, withdrawal symptoms include (but are not limited to) trouble sleeping, hands trembling, hallucinations (seeing, feeling, or hearing things that are not really there), or feeling anxious. 51 For alcohol use disorder, for example, this criterion involves the use of alcohol, sedatives, or tranquilizers to get over or avoid alcohol withdrawal symptoms.

[^34]:    52 In NSDUH data products before 2024, respondents who had a heroin use disorder or prescription pain reliever use disorder were classified as having an opioid use disorder.

[^35]:    ${ }^{53}$ This number does not include respondents whose status as past year alcohol users was unknown based on their questionnaire responses but who were statistically imputed to be past year alcohol users.

[^36]:    54 The question about receipt of overdose reversal medicine such as Narcan ${ }^{\circledR}$ or naloxone was added for 2024. Estimates based on this question are not available for prior years.

[^37]:    55 The question about the receipt of overdose reversal medicine added for 2024 was not included in the criteria asking whether respondents sought or thought they should get professional counseling, medication, or other treatment or additional professional counseling, medication, or other treatment. Beginning in 2024, respondents who reported receiving overdose reversal medication but no other treatment or services were still asked whether they sought or thought they should get professional counseling, medication, or other treatment. Likewise, respondents who did not receive substance use treatment but they received overdose reversal medicine and services from a support group, from a peer support specialist or recovery coach, in an emergency room, or from withdrawal management services were still asked whether they sought or thought they should get additional professional counseling, medication, or other treatment.

[^38]:    56 Structured Clinical Interview for the DSM-IV-TR Axis I Disorders, Research Version, Non-patient Edition (SCID-I/NP); clinical interviews would require the use of a DSM-5 diagnostic assessment to identify mental disorders according to DSM-5 criteria (First et al., 2002).

[^39]:    57 The GAF is a numeric scale used by mental health clinicians to quantify the severity of mental disorders and the extent to which mental disorders negatively affected a person's daily functioning. In the MHSS, GAF scores were assigned by clinical interviewers at the end of each SCID interview based on information gathered throughout the interview about symptoms of mental disorders and related impairment. This procedure differs from use of the WHODAS in NSDUH, which relies on respondents' (rather than clinicians') perceptions of the extent to which their symptoms of psychological distress affected their day-to-day functioning.

[^40]:    ${ }^{58}$ In the question about serious thoughts of suicide (SUI01), "[DATEFILL]" refers to the date at the start of a respondent's 12-month reference period. The interview program sets the start of the 12-month reference period as the same month and day as the interview date but in the previous calendar year.

[^41]:    ${ }^{59}$ Both the lifetime and past year measures of MDE in adults (see Section 3.4.12) were used in poststratification.
    ${ }^{60}$ Past year MDE was estimated based on responses to the SCID from the MHSS respondents and on responses from all adults to the main survey (see Section 3.4.12). These two measures were created independently. The reference here is to the SCID measure from the MHSS.

[^42]:    ${ }^{61}$ In this situation, the past year MDE measure is from the main NSDUH interview (i.e., not from the SCID).

[^43]:    62 See the 2024 Detailed Tables (CBHSQ, 2025h).

[^44]:    ${ }^{63}$ Names of questions are shown for adolescents, but wordings of the GAD-7 questions were the same for adolescents and adults.

[^45]:    64 "An MDE" refers to the occurrence of at least one MDE, rather than only one MDE. Similarly, reference to "the MDE" in a given period (e.g., the past 12 months) does not mean an individual had only one MDE in that period.
    ${ }^{65}$ Adolescent respondents aged 12 to 17 who reported that they gained weight were asked whether they gained weight because they were growing. Adult respondents aged 18 or older who reported that they gained weight were not asked this follow-up question.

[^46]:    ${ }^{66}$ These codes are also known as the Beale Codes.

[^47]:    ${ }^{67}$ Misuse of prescription drugs was broadly defined in NSDUH as use in any way a doctor did not direct people to use prescription drugs.

[^48]:    68 Although methamphetamine is available in the United States in prescription form (Desoxyn ${ }^{\circledR}$ ), most methamphetamine used in the United States is produced in clandestine laboratories rather than by the pharmaceutical industry.

[^49]:    69 Examples of ways that were presented to respondents included (1) use without a prescription of one's own; (2) use in greater amounts, more often, or longer than told to take a drug; and (3) use in any other way not directed by a doctor.

[^50]:    ${ }^{71}$ Beginning in 2024, reports of the misuse of other prescription drugs in a given category (e.g., pain relievers) were checked for reports of drugs that were not OTC drugs but were not considered to be prescription drugs.     72 For example, respondents who specified Vicodin ${ }^{\circledR}$ as the only other prescription pain reliever they misused in the past year were included in estimates for the past year misuse of hydrocodone products but were not included in estimates for the past year misuse of any other pain reliever.

[^52]:    ${ }^{76}$ Product label information for Flexeril ${ }^{\circledR}$ is available on the FDA Center for Drug Evaluation and Research website. The product label for generic cyclobenzaprine is not available on the FDA website.

[^53]:    ${ }^{77}$ For example, the product label for Xanax ${ }^{\circledR}$, which is prescribed as a tranquilizer, indicates the drug has an average half-life of 11.2 hours (i.e., the length of time for half of the dosage of the drug to be metabolized), with a range of 6.3 to 26.9 hours in healthy adults. In comparison, the product label for Halcion ${ }^{\circledR}$, which is a benzodiazepine prescribed as a sedative, has a short half-life in the range of 1.5 to 5.5 hours. Product label information for these drugs is available on the FDA Center for Drug Evaluation and Research website.
    ${ }^{78}$ When a drug is metabolized, it is converted into metabolites, which are the substances that remain after the drug is broken down by the body. For more information, see the definition for "metabolite" by typing this word as a search term on the MedlinePlus web page.

[^54]:    79 Measures for the use of IMF also have been imputed since 2022. However, estimates in the Results from the 2024 National Survey on Drug Use and Health: Detailed Tables (CBHSQ, 2025h) for the use and misuse of pain reliever subtypes included prescription fentanyl products but not IMF.

[^55]:    ${ }^{80}$ The estimated total will be lower than the true population total if the negative bias from excluding respondents with missing data outweighed other potential sources of random error (e.g., sampling error resulting from the selection of a sample) or nonrandom error (e.g., overreporting of the characteristic) that affected estimated totals in a positive direction.
    ${ }^{81}$ An exception to this general principle applied to respondents who specified they misused one or more prescription drugs for a given subtype as some "other" prescription drug they misused in the past year. For example, suppose respondents answered "don't know" when presented with the list of alprazolam products for any use in the past year. If these respondents reported the misuse of "other" tranquilizers in the past year and then specified an alprazolam product (e.g., Xanax ${ }^{\circledR}$ ) was one of the other prescription tranquilizers they misused in the past year, then these respondents logically misused alprazolam products in the past year. These respondents also logically used alprazolam products in the past year for any reason.

[^56]:    ${ }^{82}$ As discussed in Section 3.4.7, NSDUH respondents beginning in 2021 who reported any use of prescription psychotherapeutic drugs (i.e., pain relievers, tranquilizers, stimulants, or sedatives) in the past year (i.e., not just misuse of prescription drugs) were asked the respective SUD questions for that category of prescription drugs. However, tolerance and withdrawal are normal physiological adaptations when people use these prescription drugs appropriately under medical supervision or abruptly discontinue use (Hasin et al., 2013).

[^57]:    83 "Off label" prescribing refers to the prescribing of a drug that has been approved for use in the United States, but the drug is being prescribed for a condition the drug is not approved to treat (FDA, 2018).

[^58]:    ${ }^{84}$ Logically, these respondents also used prescription opioids for any reason in the past year.

[^59]:    ${ }^{85}$ That is, respondents had no missing data for the past year use of specific drugs in the 11 prescription opioid subtypes in the NSDUH questionnaire.
